These are core platform capabilities that come out of the box with Neon.
High availability.
Neon’s storage is
multi-AZ
by default, without the need for HA standbys.
Read replicas.
Neon lets you offload
read-heavy queries
without added storage costs.
Autoscaling.
Neon automatically
adjusts compute
and storage based on demand, including on the Free plan.
Monitoring.
Built-in dashboards in the console give you
real-time
visibility into usage and performance.
Connection pooling.
All Neon databases can use pooled connections built on pgBouncer (up to
10,000 connections
).
Postgres extensions library.
You can add extensions like
pg_vector
,
PostGIS
,
TimescaleDB
, and many more.
Managed Better Auth.
Neon comes with
built-in authentication
based on Better Auth, with a one-click install.
GDPR and ISO compliance.
Included across all plans. Learn more on our
Security page
.
Your questions,
answered
Does Neon offer a backend now?
Yes. Alongside Lakebase Postgres, Neon now offers Managed Better Auth, Object Storage, Functions, and AI Gateway. Together they form a full backend suite that runs alongside your database and branches with it.
What is a Neon project?
A project in Neon is the top-level container for your environment. Each project includes all branches, with your Postgres databases and (if you deploy them) your Object Storage buckets, Functions, Managed Better Auth endpoints, and AI Gateway access.
What is a CU?
A CU (short for Compute Unit) is Neon's way of representing
Postgres database compute size
. Neon databases
autoscale
, and CUs define how much CPU and memory your database is using at any moment. Each CU allocates approximately 4 GB of RAM, along with associated CPU.
What is a CU-hour?
A CU-hour is Neon's unit for measuring
Postgres database compute usage
. Because Neon databases scale to zero and autoscale, you're billed only for the database compute resources you actually use. In other words, your monthly database compute usage depends on:
How many compute endpoints you're running
How large your compute endpoints are (in CUs)
How long they run (in hours)
For a single compute, the calculation works like this:
Monthly CU-hours = [average CU size] × [number of hours the compute runs in the month]
Monthly compute cost = [monthly CU-hours] × [CU-hour price]
The CU-hour price depends on your pricing plan:
Launch plan: $0.106 per CU-hour
Scale plan: $0.222 per CU-hour
Remember that when your database is idle, compute can scale down to zero, so you don't consume CU-hours while it's not running.
Learn more about Neon's billing.
What does 100 CU-hours per project give me on the Free plan?
On the Free plan, each project gets 100 CU-hours of
database compute
per month, and compute automatically scales down to zero when idle. What this means in practice:
You can create up to 100 projects, each with its own independent 100 CU-hour allowance
CU-hours are only consumed while a database is actively running queries. When a project is idle, compute scales to zero and uses no CU-hours
Usage is typically spread across short bursts of activity, not continuous runtime
A realistic example for a single project:
The database runs at 0.25 CU, the default compute size for Free plan projects, for ~12 hours per day. It scales down to zero the rest of the time
Over a month, this uses roughly 90 CU-hours (0.25 CU × 12 hours × 30 days), staying within the Free plan limits for that project
At 0.25 CU, 100 CU-hours covers about 400 hours of active compute, more than half the hours in a month
This model is designed to make it practical to have many small or intermittent databases (for development, demos, previews, or experiments) without being forced into an upgrade.
Learn more about our Free plan philosophy.
Is Object Storage, Functions, or AI Gateway usage included in my Free plan CU-hours?
No. CU-hours measure Postgres database compute only. Object Storage, Functions, and AI Gateway have their own allowances and rates, shown in the plan table.
How are the workload cost estimates calculated?
The typical monthly cost estimates shown in our pricing plans are based on representative database workloads that reflect common usage patterns across different application types. These estimates cover Postgres compute and storage only. They don't factor in Object Storage, Functions, or AI Gateway usage, which are billed separately.
Each estimate combines two components:
Database compute cost:
Based on the specified CU-hours per month.
Database storage cost:
Based on the specified amount of database storage in GB.
These estimates help you compare typical workloads across plans.
Here's a detailed breakdown of each of the workload sizes and what they represent:
Intermittent load
140 CU-hours compute, 1 GB-month of storage. Typical of a small database that doesn't need to run 24/7.
A database using 140 CU-hours compute per month can scale to zero when idle, and start back up within a few hundred milliseconds when it's needed. When it's running it is set to a minimum size of 0.25 CU but can scale up to 2 CU when needed.
Low load
190 CU-hours compute, 5 GB-month of storage. A small database that
does
need to run 24/7.
Once scale to zero is disabled, the minimum CU-hours a database can use in a month is about 182.5
(about 730 hours in a month × 0.25 minimum CU size ≈ 182.5)
, so this example represents a database that has scale to zero disabled but only occasionally needs to scale above the 0.25 minimum size. Good for small production applications and databases that need to be always-on for features like real-time sync.
Medium load
720 CU-hours compute, 10 GB-month of storage. Representative of a constant-load application database.
As you can see from the autoscaling chart above, 720 CU hours translates to a database that scales between 0.5 and 4 CU based on load.
High load
3,000 CU-hours compute, 100 GB-month of storage.
As an example, a database that is scaling between 3 CU and 7 CU will often use around 3000 CU hours in a month. This level represents production applications with sustained traffic, higher data volumes, and more complex queries requiring consistent compute resources.
XL load
6,000 CU-hours compute, 1,000 GB-month of storage.
A database scaling between 6 and 14 CU can use in the range of 6,000 CU hours in a month. This is representative of large-scale production workloads with high traffic volumes, extensive datasets, and applications requiring significant compute capacity and storage.
Your actual costs will vary based on:
How much your database autoscales based on actual query load
How long your computes run before scaling to zero
Your actual data size and number of branches
Your configured history window for instant restore (affects History storage costs)
Remember that Neon bills based on actual usage, not estimates. Use these workload examples as reference points, then monitor your usage in the
Neon Console
to understand your real costs.
Learn more about how Neon billing works.
How are Object Storage, Functions, and AI Gateway billed?
Each is billed separately from Postgres compute (CU-hours).
Object Storage
is $0.023 per GB-month of stored volume. There's no per-operation charge. Egress counts toward your public network transfer allowance. The Free plan includes 5 GB of Object Storage per project.
Functions
are billed for active compute, waiting compute, and invocations at the rates in the plan table. The Free plan includes 10 active capacity-hours, 400 waiting capacity-hours, and 1 million invocations per project per month.
AI Gateway
is available on Launch and Scale. Pricing matches the model provider's list prices, with no markup, and draws down prepaid credits.
Learn how prepaid credits work
, or see
per-model rates
.
What does "no monthly minimum" mean on paid plans?
Neon's paid plans don't require a minimum monthly spend or base fee. You're billed purely based on usage. If one month you barely use Neon, your bill might be just a few dollars.
For example, if your databases are mostly idle that month, or you only run them briefly for development or testing, you'll only pay for the compute and storage actually consumed during that time. Remember that Neon databases scale to zero by default.
Are the Free plan quotas for database usage included in the paid plans?
No. When you're on a paid plan, database usage starts metering from zero for both compute and storage. Free plan quotas don't apply once you upgrade. Instead, you're billed purely based on actual usage, at the rates of your paid plan.
You should consider a paid plan if:
You expect higher or more sustained usage than what's included per project on the Free plan
You care about production guarantees, such as consistent availability, predictable performance, and never having your database paused due to usage limits
If your usage fits comfortably within the Free plan and you're not running a production workload, there's no reason to upgrade. The Free plan is designed to be genuinely useful for development and prototyping.
Learn more about our Free plan philosophy.
How is Postgres database storage billed in Neon?
In Neon, you don't provision or manage database storage in advance. It scales automatically and invisibly as your data grows. At the end of each month, you're billed for the database storage actually consumed per project, measured in GB-months.
Neon bills storage usage using two separate metrics:
History storage (or instant restore storage)
Instant restore
relies on Neon retaining a history of database changes so you can restore a branch to a previous point in time, create branches from past states, run Time Travel queries, and more. How long that history is kept is controlled by the
history window
on
Settings → Instant restore
(1 day by default on paid plans). See
History window
.
History
storage is billed based on the amount of Write-Ahead Log (WAL) retained within that history window, at $0.20 per GB-month on paid plans. This is billed separately from your regular database storage.
If you don't need deep recovery or long Time Travel, shorten the history window to reduce costs.
Database storage (root and child branches)
This is the storage used by your database data itself. Since Neon databases can branch, this is how branches contribute to database storage:
Root branches are billed based on their actual data size (for example, 5 GB)
Child branches
might
be billed based on the minimum of: the accumulated data changes since the branch was created, or the underlying storage footprint, which is zero if the branch is still within the history window used for instant restore (in this case, the child branch effectively shares storage with its parent).
The sum of both components will be billed as your database storage at $0.35 per GB-month.
What this implies:
A child branch that has no data changes compared to its parent and is still within the
history window
does not incur additional database storage costs.
As a child branch accumulates changes over time, its storage usage increases.
If a child branch falls out of the history window, it becomes as expensive as a root branch, since it no longer shares storage with its parent.
To keep database storage costs low, keep child branches short-lived: set
expiration times
,
reset them
frequently, or delete branches when they're no longer needed.
How are extra branches billed?
Each paid plan includes a set number of branches per project at no additional cost (10 branches per project on Launch, 25 branches per project on Scale). You can create and delete branches freely within that allowance:
If the total number of concurrent branches in a project exceeds your plan's allowance, the extra branches are billed as branch-months, prorated hourly.
The price is $1.50 per extra branch-month (≈ $0.002 per hour)
You're only billed for branches that exceed your included limit, and only for the time they exist.
Example: The Launch plan includes 10 branches per project. If you already have 10 branches but create 2 additional branches and keep them for 5 hours each, that'd be 10 extra branch-hours total: 10 × $0.002/hour = $0.02
How to avoid extra branch charges
Use branch expiration.
Set automatic deletion times on temporary branches so they're cleaned up when no longer needed.
Automate cleanup. Use the
Neon API
or
Neon CLI
to periodically delete unused branches and stay within your included allowance.
How is Managed Better Auth billed?
Neon Managed Better Auth is included at no additional cost for all Neon databases until you reach 1 million monthly active users (MAU) per project. If you need more, you can request an increase in the Neon Console.
On the Free plan, Managed Better Auth is included for up to 60,000 MAU per project.
MAU limits apply to each project separately. For example, three projects on the Free plan can each have up to 60,000 MAU.
How can I control my Postgres database costs (compute and storage consumption)?
Database compute is often the most variable part of a monthly bill. For Neon databases, the most effective way to control compute costs is to configure maximum autoscaling limits and scale-to-zero.
Autoscaling limits act as a built-in cost ceiling: your database will never scale beyond the limit you set, even during traffic spikes. If you want to prioritize performance over costs in a particular compute endpoint (e.g. production), choose a higher limit. If you want to optimize for cost predictability, set a lower one.
Learn how to configure autoscaling limits.
Another effective way to control database compute costs is to ensure scale to zero is enabled for all non-production branches. When a branch is idle, compute scales down automatically, so you don't pay for compute while it's idle.
Learn about scale to zero.
To manage database storage costs, regularly clean up unused branches, snapshots, and projects, and avoid retaining a large history window for instant restore if your use case does not require it.
Learn more about optimizing storage usage.
Do you offer credit programs for startups?
Early startups that have received venture funding are eligible to apply to our Startup Program.
Learn more and apply here.

compute\u003c/a\u003e and storage based on demand, including on the Free plan.\"}}]]}]]}],\"$L9b\",\"$L9c\",\"$L9d\",\"$L9e\",\"$L9f\"]}]]}]}]\na1:T477,\n      \u003cp\u003eA CU-hour is Neon's unit for measuring \u003cstrong\u003ePostgres database compute usage\u003c/strong\u003e. Because Neon databases scale to zero and autoscale, you're billed only for the database compute resources you actually use. In other words, your monthly database compute usage depends on:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eHow many compute endpoints you're running\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eHow large your compute endpoints are (in CUs)\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eHow long they run (in hours)\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eFor a single compute, the calculation works like this:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003ccode\u003eMonthly CU-hours = [average CU size] × [number of hours the compute runs in the month]\u003c/code\u003e\n      \u003ccode\u003eMonthly compute cost = [monthly CU-hours] × [CU-hour price]\u003c/code\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eThe CU-hour price depends on your pricing plan:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eLaunch plan: $0.106 per CU-hour\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eScale plan: $0.222 per CU-hour\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eRemember that when your database is idle, compute can scale down to zero, so you don't consume CU-hours while it's not running. \u003ca href=\"/docs/introduction/about-billing\"\u003eLearn more about Neon's billing.\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n    a2:T56f,\n      \u003cp\u003eOn the Free plan, each project gets 100 CU-hours of \u003cstrong\u003edatabase compute\u003c/strong\u003e per month, and compute automatically scales down to zero when idle. What this means in practice:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eYou can create up to 100 projects, each with its own independent 100 CU-hour allowance\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eCU-hours are only consumed while a database is actively running queries. When a project is idle, compute scales to zero and uses no CU-hours\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eUsage is typically spread across short bursts of activity, not continuous runtime\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eA realistic example for a single project:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eThe database runs at 0.25 CU, the default compute size for Free plan projects, for ~12 hours per day. It scales down to zero the rest of the time\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eOver a month, this uses roughly 90 CU-hours (0.25 CU × 12 hours × 30 days), staying within the Free plan limits for that project\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eAt 0.25 CU, 100 CU-hours covers about 400 hours of active compute, more than half the hours in a month\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eThis model is designed to make it practical to have many small or intermittent databases (for development, demos, previews, or experiments) without being forced into an upgrade. \u003ca href=\"/blog/why-so-many-projects-in-the-neon-free-plan\"\u003eLearn more about our Free plan philosophy.\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n    45:[\"$\",\"section\",null,{\"className\":\"faq safe-paddings mt-[200px] xl:mt-[184px] lg:mt-40 md:mt-[104px]\",\"id\":\"faq\",\"children\":[\"$\",\"div\",null,{\"className\":\"relative mx-auto lg:max-w-none lg:px-8 md:px-5 max-w-6xl px-8 flex gap-x-16 xl:gap-x-8 lg:flex-col lg:gap-y-10 md:gap-y-5\",\"ref\":\"$undefined\",\"children\":[[\"$\",\"h2\",null,{\"className\":\"w-80 shrink-0 text-[44px] leading-dense tracking-[-0.05em] text-balance xl:w-[288px] xl:text-[36px] lg:w-full md:text-[28px]\",\"children\":[\"Your questions,\",\" \",[\"$\",\"br\",null,{\"className\":\"lg:hidden\"}],\"answered\",null]}],[\"$\",\"ul\",null,{\"className\":\"flex w-full flex-col\",\"children\":[[\"$\",\"$La0\",\"Does Neon offer a backend now?\",{\"question\":\"Does Neon offer a backend now?\",\"id\":\"does-neon-offer-a-backend\",\"initialState\":\"open\",\"answer\":\"\\n      \u003cp\u003eYes. They don't factor in Object Storage, Functions, or AI Gateway usage, which are billed separately.\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eEach estimate combines two components:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDatabase compute cost:\u003c/strong\u003e Based on the specified CU-hours per month. \u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDatabase storage cost:\u003c/strong\u003e Based on the specified amount of database storage in GB. \u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eThese estimates help you compare typical workloads across plans.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eHere's a detailed breakdown of each of the workload sizes and what they represent:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003ch3 class=\"mt-6 text-lg text-white font-semibold\"\u003eIntermittent load\u003c/h3\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\n        140 CU-hours compute, 1 GB-month of storage. Typical of a small database that doesn't need to run 24/7.\n        \u003cimg class=\"my-4\" src=\"/images/pricing/140-CU-Hours.webp\" /\u003e\n        A database using 140 CU-hours compute per month can scale to zero when idle, and start back up within a few hundred milliseconds when it's needed. When it's running it is set to a minimum size of 0.25 CU but can scale up to 2 CU when needed.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003ch3 class=\"mt-6 text-lg text-white font-semibold\"\u003eLow load\u003c/h3\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\n        190 CU-hours compute, 5 GB-month of storage. A small database that \u003cem\u003edoes\u003c/em\u003e need to run 24/7.\n        \u003cimg class=\"my-4\" src=\"/images/pricing/190-CU-Hours.webp\" /\u003e\n        Once scale to zero is disabled, the minimum CU-hours a database can use in a month is about 182.5 \u003cem\u003e(about 730 hours in a month × 0.25 minimum CU size ≈ 182.5)\u003c/em\u003e, so this example represents a database that has scale to zero disabled but only occasionally needs to scale above the 0.25 minimum size. Good for small production applications and databases that need to be always-on for features like real-time sync.\n      \u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003ch3 class=\"mt-6 text-lg text-white font-semibold\"\u003eMedium load\u003c/h3\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\n        720 CU-hours compute, 10 GB-month of storage. Representative of a constant-load application database.\n        \u003cimg class=\"my-4\" src=\"/images/pricing/720-CU-Hours.webp\" /\u003e\n        As you can see from the autoscaling chart above, 720 CU hours translates to a database that scales between 0.5 and 4 CU based on load. \n      \u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003ch3 class=\"mt-6 text-lg text-white font-semibold\"\u003eHigh load\u003c/h3\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\n        3,000 CU-hours compute, 100 GB-month of storage.\n        \u003cimg class=\"my-4\" src=\"/images/pricing/3000-CU-Hours.webp\" /\u003e\n        As an example, a database that is scaling between 3 CU and 7 CU will often use around 3000 CU hours in a month. This level represents production applications with sustained traffic, higher data volumes, and more complex queries requiring consistent compute resources.\n      \u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003ch3 class=\"mt-6 text-lg text-white font-semibold\"\u003eXL load\u003c/h3\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\n        6,000 CU-hours compute, 1,000 GB-month of storage.\n        \u003cimg class=\"my-4\" src=\"/images/pricing/6000-CU-Hours.webp\" /\u003e\n        A database scaling between 6 and 14 CU can use in the range of 6,000 CU hours in a month. This is representative of large-scale production workloads with high traffic volumes, extensive datasets, and applications requiring significant compute capacity and storage.\n      \u003c/p\u003e\n      \n      \u003cp\u003eYour actual costs will vary based on:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eHow much your database autoscales based on actual query load\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eHow long your computes run before scaling to zero\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eYour actual data size and number of branches\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eYour configured history window for instant restore (affects History storage costs)\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eRemember that Neon bills based on actual usage, not estimates. Use these workload examples as reference points, then monitor your usage in the \u003ca href=\"https://console.neon.tech\"\u003eNeon Console\u003c/a\u003e to understand your real costs. \u003ca href=\"/docs/introduction/about-billing\"\u003eLearn more about how Neon billing works.\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n    a4:[\"$\",\"$La0\",\"How are the workload cost estimates calculated?\",{\"question\":\"How are the workload cost estimates calculated?\",\"id\":\"workload-cost-estimates\",\"answer\":\"$10d\",\"index\":6,\"variant\":\"default\"}]\na5:[\"$\",\"$La0\",\"How are Object Storage, Functions, and AI Gateway billed?\",{\"question\":\"How are Object Storage, Functions, and AI Gateway billed?\",\"id\":\"backend-billing\",\"answer\":\"\\n      \u003cp\u003eEach is billed separately from Postgres compute (CU-hours).\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eObject Storage\u003c/strong\u003e is $0.023 per GB-month of stored volume. There's no per-operation charge. Egress counts toward your public network transfer allowance. The Free plan includes 5 GB of Object Storage per project.\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eFunctions\u003c/strong\u003e are billed for active compute, waiting compute, and invocations at the rates in the plan table. The Free plan includes 10 active capacity-hours, 400 waiting capacity-hours, and 1 million invocations per project per month.\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAI Gateway\u003c/strong\u003e is available on Launch and Scale. Pricing matches the model provider's list prices, with no markup, and draws down prepaid credits. \u003ca href=\\\"/docs/ai-gateway/prepaid-credits\\\"\u003eLearn how prepaid credits work\u003c/a\u003e, or see \u003ca href=\\\"/docs/ai-gateway/overview#available-models\\\"\u003eper-model rates\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\\n    \",\"index\":7,\"variant\":\"default\"}]\na6:[\"$\",\"$La0\",\"What does \\\"no monthly minimum\\\" mean on paid plans?\",{\"question\":\"What does \\\"no monthly minimum\\\" mean on paid plans?\",\"answer\":\"\\n      \u003cp\u003eNeon's paid plans don't require a minimum monthly spend or base fee. You're billed purely based on usage. If one month you barely use Neon, your bill might be just a few dollars.\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003eFor example, if your databases are mostly idle that month, or you only run them briefly for development or testing, you'll only pay for the compute and storage actually consumed during that time. Remember that Neon databases scale to zero by default.\u003c/p\u003e\\n    \",\"index\":8,\"variant\":\"default\"}]\na7:[\"$\",\"$La0\",\"Are the Free plan quotas for database usage included in the paid plans?\",{\"question\":\"Are the Free plan quotas for database usage included in the paid plans?\",\"answer\":\"\\n      \u003cp\u003eNo. When you're on a paid plan, database usage starts metering from zero for both compute and storage. Free plan quotas don't apply once you upgrade. Instead, you're billed purely based on actual usage, at the rates of your paid plan.\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003eYou should consider a paid plan if:\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cul\u003e\\n        \u003cli\u003eYou expect higher or more sustained usage than what's included per project on the Free plan\u003c/li\u003e\\n        \u003cli\u003eYou care about production guarantees, such as consistent availability, predictable performance, and never having your database paused due to usage limits\u003c/li\u003e\\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003eIf your usage fits comfortably within the Free plan and you're not running a production workload, there's no reason to upgrade. The Free plan is designed to be genuinely useful for development and prototyping. \u003ca href=\\\"/blog/why-so-many-projects-in-the-neon-free-plan\\\"\u003eLearn more about our Free plan philosophy.\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\\n    \",\"index\":9,\"variant\":\"default\"}]\n10e:Taed,\n      \u003cp\u003eIn Neon, you don't provision or manage database storage in advance. It scales automatically and invisibly as your data grows. At the end of each month, you're billed for the database storage actually consumed per project, measured in GB-months.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eNeon bills storage usage using two separate metrics:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHistory storage (or instant restore storage)\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInstant restore\u003c/strong\u003e relies on Neon retaining a history of database changes so you can restore a branch to a previous point in time, create branches from past states, run Time Travel queries, and more. How long that history is kept is controlled by the \u003cstrong\u003ehistory window\u003c/strong\u003e on \u003cstrong\u003eSettings → Instant restore\u003c/strong\u003e (1 day by default on paid plans). See \u003ca href=\"/docs/introduction/history-window\"\u003eHistory window\u003c/a\u003e.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHistory\u003c/strong\u003e storage is billed based on the amount of Write-Ahead Log (WAL) retained within that history window, at $0.20 per GB-month on paid plans. This is billed separately from your regular database storage.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eIf you don't need deep recovery or long Time Travel, shorten the history window to reduce costs.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDatabase storage (root and child branches)\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eThis is the storage used by your database data itself. Since Neon databases can branch, this is how branches contribute to database storage:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eRoot branches are billed based on their actual data size (for example, 5 GB)\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eChild branches \u003cem\u003emight\u003c/em\u003e be billed based on the minimum of: the accumulated data changes since the branch was created, or the underlying storage footprint, which is zero if the branch is still within the history window used for instant restore (in this case, the child branch effectively shares storage with its parent).\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eThe sum of both components will be billed as your database storage at $0.35 per GB-month.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eWhat this implies:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eA child branch that has no data changes compared to its parent and is still within the \u003ca href=\"/docs/introduction/history-window\"\u003ehistory window\u003c/a\u003e does not incur additional database storage costs.\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eAs a child branch accumulates changes over time, its storage usage increases.\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eIf a child branch falls out of the history window, it becomes as expensive as a root branch, since it no longer shares storage with its parent.\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eTo keep database storage costs low, keep child branches short-lived: set \u003ca href=\"/docs/guides/branch-expiration\"\u003eexpiration times\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"/docs/guides/reset-from-parent\"\u003ereset them\u003c/a\u003e frequently, or delete branches when they're no longer needed.\u003c/p\u003e\n    a8:[\"$\",\"$La0\",\"How is Postgres database storage billed in Neon?\",{\"question\":\"How is Postgres database storage billed in Neon?\",\"id\":\"branches-and-storage\",\"answer\":\"$10e\",\"index\":10,\"variant\":\"default\"}]\n10f:T566,\n      \u003cp\u003eEach paid plan includes a set number of branches per project at no additional cost (10 branches per project on Launch, 25 branches per project on Scale). You can create and delete branches freely within that allowance:\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eIf the total number of concurrent branches in a project exceeds your plan's allowance, the extra branches are billed as branch-months, prorated hourly.\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eThe price is $1.50 per extra branch-month (≈ $0.002 per hour)\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eYou're only billed for branches that exceed your included limit, and only for the time they exist.\u003c/li\u003e\n      \u003c/ul\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eExample: The Launch plan includes 10 branches per project. If you already have 10 branches but create 2 additional branches and keep them for 5 hours each, that'd be 10 extra branch-hours total: 10 × $0.002/hour = $0.02\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eHow to avoid extra branch charges\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cul\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003e\u003ca href=\"/docs/guides/branch-expiration\"\u003eUse branch expiration.\u003c/a\u003e Set automatic deletion times on temporary branches so they're cleaned up when no longer needed.\u003c/li\u003e\n        \u003cli\u003eAutomate cleanup. If you need more, you can request an increase in the Neon Console.\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003eOn the Free plan, Managed Better Auth is included for up to 60,000 MAU per project.\u003c/p\u003e\\n      \u003cp\u003eMAU limits apply to each project separately. For Neon databases, the most effective way to control compute costs is to configure maximum autoscaling limits and scale-to-zero.\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eAutoscaling limits act as a built-in cost ceiling: your database will never scale beyond the limit you set, even during traffic spikes. If you want to prioritize performance over costs in a particular compute endpoint (e.g. production), choose a higher limit. If you want to optimize for cost predictability, set a lower one. \u003ca href=\"/docs/guides/autoscaling-guide#configure-autoscaling-defaults-for-your-project\"\u003eLearn how to configure autoscaling limits.\u003c/a\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\n      \u003cp\u003eAnother effective way to control database compute costs is to ensure scale to zero is enabled for all non-production branches.
